\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{1}(I_{4}\otimes W)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}))&\subset&\mbox{Ad}_{u_{3}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(2)}U_{1}\big)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{10}\oplus M_{5}\oplus\mathbb{C}))\\
\end{matrix}

For
k=2:

\begin{matrix}\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}&\subset&\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{3}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(2)}U_{1}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{10}\oplus M_{5}\oplus\mathbb{C})\big)&\subset&\mbox{Ad}_{u_{5}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(3)}U_{2}\big)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{35}\oplus M_{21}\oplus M_{7}\oplus\mathbb{C}))\\
\end{matrix}

For
k=3:

\begin{matrix}\mbox{Ad}_{u_{7}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(4)}U_{3}\big)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{126}\oplus M_{84}\oplus M_{36}\oplus M_{9}\oplus\mathbb{C}))&\subset&\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{5}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(3)}U_{2}\big)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{35}\oplus M_{21}\oplus M_{7}\oplus\mathbb{C}))&\subset&\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}\\
\end{matrix}

Lemma 7.27
.

For
u\nsim v
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the inclusion matrix for the inclusion
\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\subset M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}
is the column matrix
(1\,\,1\,\ldots\,1)^{T}
of order
(k+2)\times 1
.

Proof
:
By
7.20
, we have the spectral decomposition of the unitary matrix
(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Suppose that
k
is odd. Then,
\{1,\theta^{\,2},\overline{\theta}^{\,2},\ldots,\theta^{\,k+1},\overline{\theta}^{\,k+1}\}
is the set of distinct eigenvalues of
(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}
, and
m_{k}(1)+\sum_{r}m_{k}(\theta^{\,2r})+m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})=4^{k+1}
, where
m_{k}(.)
are the multiplicities of the eigenvalues. By
7.26
it is now clear that the required inclusion matrix is the column matrix
(1\,\,1\,\ldots\,1)^{T}
of order
(k+2)\times 1
, as there are
(k+2)
-many eigenvalues of
(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}
by
7.15
. Exactly similar analysis holds for
k
even.

\Box

Lemma 7.28
.

For
u\nsim v
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{k}
for the inclusion

C_{2k+1}:=\mathrm{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\cap\mathrm{Ad}_{v_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}=:A_{2k+1}

is the
(k+2)\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
. Thus,
||\Lambda_{k}||^{2}=4(k+2)
.

Proof
:
Since
\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}
is the direct sum of four copies of
M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}
, and the unitary matrix
u_{2k+1}(I_{4}\otimes W^{(k+1)}U_{k})
in
7.26
lies in
\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}
, each entry of the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{k}
equals to
1
is clear by
7.27
. To determine
||\Lambda_{k}||^{2}
, observe that
\Lambda_{k}\Lambda_{k}^{T}
is the matrix
4(k+2)J_{k+2}
, where
J_{k+2}
is the projection
\sum_{j=1}^{k+2}(k+2)^{-1}E_{jj}
in
M_{k+2}
.

\Box

Remark 7.29
.

In the following tower of commuting squares

\begin{matrix}A_{1}&\subset&A_{3}&\subset&A_{5}&\subset&\ldots\cr\cup&&\cup&&\cup&&&\cr C_{1}&\subset&C_{3}&\subset&C_{5}&\subset&\ldots\end{matrix}

the first quadruple
(C_{1}\subset A_{1},C_{3}\subset A_{3})
is not non-degenerate. This is because the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{C_{1}}^{A_{1}}=\Lambda_{0}
is a
2\times 4
matrix with
||\Lambda_{C_{1}}^{A_{1}}||^{2}=8
by
7.28
, whereas the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{C_{3}}^{A_{3}}=\Lambda_{1}
is a
3\times 4
matrix with
||\Lambda_{C_{3}}^{A_{3}}||^{2}=12
. That is,
||\Lambda_{C_{1}}^{A_{1}}||^{2}\neq||\Lambda_{C_{3}}^{A_{3}}||^{2}
. In fact, none of the quadruples are non-degenerate, which follows from
7.28
. This also justifies part
(ii)
in
3.4
. Moreover, we can also conclude that
C_{1}\subset C_{3}\subset C_{5}\subset\ldots
is not a basic construction, as otherwise the quadruple
(C_{1}\subset A_{1},C_{3}\subset A_{3})
would have been non-degenerate by
[
5
]
.

Theorem 7.30
.

If
u\nsim v
, then
\lambda(R,R_{u}\cap R_{v})=\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})=\lambda(R_{v},R_{u}\cap R_{v})=0
. That is,
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is a von Neumann subalgebra of the hyperfinite
II_{1}
factor
R
with infinite Pimsner-Popa index.

Proof
:
First observe that it is enough to only conclude that
\lambda(R,R_{u}\cap R_{v})=0
. This is because by the submultiplicativity property of
\lambda
for
R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u}\subset R
, we have
\lambda(R,R_{u}\cap R_{v})\geq\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})\lambda(R,R_{u})=\frac{1}{4}\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})
(similarly for
v
in place of
u
) because
\lambda(R,R_{u})=[R:R_{u}]^{-1}=\frac{1}{4}
by
[
36
]
. Hence if
\lambda(R,R_{u}\cap R_{v})=0
, then automatically we get that
\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})=0
.

Now by
2.24
, we have the following

\lambda\left(\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\,,\,\mathrm{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\cap\mathrm{Ad}_{v_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\right)^{-1}=\max_{\ell}\Big(\sum_{r=1}^{k+2}\frac{b_{r\ell}s_{r}}{t_{\ell}}\Big)\,,

where
b_{r\ell}=\min\{a_{r\ell},n_{r}\}
and
s_{r}
denotes the trace of minimal projections in each summand
N_{r}
of
\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}
described in
7.25
, and
t_{\ell}
denotes trace of minimal projections in
\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,4
. Hence,
t_{\ell}=\frac{1}{4^{(k+2)}}
for each
\ell
(recall the unique trace on
R
obtained through the basic construction as in
Figure

11
), and since the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{k}
is the
(k+2)\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
, we get that
b_{r\ell}=1
for each
r,\ell
. This says that
s_{r}=\frac{1}{4^{(k+1)}}
for each
1\leq r\leq k+2
because
\Lambda_{k}(t_{1}\,\,t_{2}\,\,t_{3}\,\,t_{4})^{T}=(s_{1}\,\,\cdots\,\,s_{k+2})^{T}
. Thus,

\lambda^{-1}=4^{(k+2)}\sum_{r=1}^{k+2}s_{r}=4^{(k+2)}\sum_{r=1}^{k+2}\frac{1}{4^{(k+1)}}=4(k+2)\,.

Recall that
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is the SOT-limit of
C_{2k+1}=\mathrm{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\cap\mathrm{Ad}_{v_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)
. Now, using
2.13
we get that
\lambda(R,R_{u}\cap R_{v})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{4(k+2)}=0
.

\Box

Therefore, we obtain the following family of von Neumann subalgebras of
R

\left\{R_{u(a)}\cap R_{u(b)}\subset R:\,u(a)\nsim u(b);\,a,b\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\right\}\,,

such that any member of this family has infinite Pimsner-Popa index in
R
.

Question:
Is
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
for
u\nsim v
a factor?

7.2.3
Construction of a family of finite index subfactors of
R

We now deal with the case of
u\sim v
. Before we jump into the general situation, first let us understand few initial cases to realize where it differs from the corresponding situation in
u\nsim v
. Recall
7.26
in this regard. Consider again the following tower of commuting squares

\begin{matrix}A_{1}&\subset&A_{3}&\subset&A_{5}&\subset&\ldots\cr\cup&&\cup&&\cup&&&\cr C_{1}&\subset&C_{3}&\subset&C_{5}&\subset&\ldots\end{matrix}

In the case of
u\nsim v
, for
k=0
, we have seen that the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{0}
for the inclusion
C_{1}=\mbox{Ad}_{u_{1}(I_{4}\otimes W)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}))\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}=A_{1}
is the
2\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
(
7.28
), and the square of its norm is
||\Lambda_{0}||^{2}=8
. Now let
k=1
. The inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{1}
for the inclusion
C_{3}=\mbox{Ad}_{u_{3}(I_{4}\otimes W^{(2)}U_{1})}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{10}\oplus M_{5}\oplus\mathbb{C}))\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}=A_{3}
is the
3\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
, and the square of its norm is
||\Lambda_{1}||^{2}=12
. Thus, the following quadruple

\begin{matrix}A_{1}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}&\subset&\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}=:A_{3}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
C_{1}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{1}(I_{4}\otimes W)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}))&\subset&\mbox{Ad}_{u_{3}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(2)}U_{1}\big)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{10}\oplus M_{5}\oplus\mathbb{C}))=:C_{3}\\
\end{matrix}

is a commuting square which is not non-degenerate if
u\nsim v
. However, if we assume that
u\sim v
and
\theta=\overline{a}b
is such that
\theta^{\,4}=1
, then the situation changes drastically. By
7.16
, we see that
\sigma\big((u_{2}^{*}v_{2})^{2}\big)=\{1,\theta^{\,2}\}
as
\theta^{\,4}=1
(putting
k=2
there). By
7.21
, the multiplicity of the eigenvalue
1
remains the same, i,e.
10
, but that of
\theta^{\,2}
is now
6
(as
\theta^{\,2}=\overline{\theta}^{\,2}
) instead of
5
. In view of
7.26
, we now have the following modified quadruple,

\begin{matrix}A_{1}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}&\subset&\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}=:A_{3}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
C_{1}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{1}(I_{4}\otimes W)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}))&\subset&\mbox{Ad}_{u_{3}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(2)}U_{1}\big)}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{10}\oplus M_{6}))=:C_{3}\\
\end{matrix}

The inclusion matrix
\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}
for the inclusion
\mbox{Ad}_{u_{3}(I_{4}\otimes W^{(2)}U_{1})}(\mathbb{C}\otimes(M_{10}\oplus M_{6}))\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}
is the
2\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
. That is,
\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}=\Lambda_{0}
and
||\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}||^{2}=||\Lambda_{0}||^{2}=8
. Therefore, in this case the quadruple
(C_{1}\subset A_{1},C_{3}\subset A_{3})
, which is already a commuting square, becomes non-degenerate also by Lemma
3.10
in
[
5
]
. Iterating Jones’ basic construction on the quadruple
(C_{1}\subset A_{1},C_{3}\subset A_{3})
, we obtain a subfactor
R^{(4)}
of
R
such that
R^{(4)}\subseteq R_{u}\cap R_{v}
(by
2.27
) and
[R:R^{(4)}]=||\Lambda_{0}||^{2}=8
. Since
[R_{u}:R^{(4)}]=2
, by the irreducibility of
R^{(4)}
in
R_{u}
, it follows that
R^{(4)}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}
. That is,
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is a finite index subfactor of
R
with
[R:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=8
. This is in sharp contrast with
7.30
.

Similarly, if we assume that
u\sim v
and
\theta=\overline{a}b
is such that
\theta^{\,6}=1
, we see that
\sigma\big((u_{2}^{*}v_{2})^{2}\big)=\sigma\big((u_{4}^{*}v_{4})^{2}\big)=\{1,\theta^{\,2},\overline{\theta}^{\,2}\}
by
7.16
. In this case, the quadruple
(C_{1}\subset A_{1},C_{3}\subset A_{3})
remains not non-degenerate as before, however, a similar analysis as above tells us that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{2}=\Lambda_{1}
with the square of their norm being equal to
12
. In other words, the quadruple
(C_{3}\subset A_{3},C_{5}\subset A_{5})
becomes a non-degenerate commuting square. Therefore, iterating Jones’ basic construction on the quadruple
(C_{3}\subset A_{3},C_{5}\subset A_{5})
, we obtain a subfactor
R^{(6)}
of
R
such that
R^{(6)}\subseteq R_{u}\cap R_{v}
(by
2.27
) and
[R:R^{(6)}]=||\Lambda_{1}||^{2}=12
. Since
[R_{u}:R^{(6)}]=3
, by the irreducibility of
R^{(6)}
in
R_{u}
, it follows that
R^{(6)}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}
. That is,
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is a finite index subfactor of
R
with
[R:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=12
, which is again in sharp contrast with
7.30
.

It turns out that this phenomena happens in general, that is, for
\theta^{\,m}=1
where
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
. We need the following technical lemma first. Recall
7.16
and
7.21
in this regard, along with the notations.

Lemma 7.31
.

For
u=u(a),v=u(b)
let
\theta=\overline{a}b
, and suppose that
\theta
is a primitive even root of unity. That is,
\,\theta^{\,m}=1
for some
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
. Then, for
k=(m-2)/2
we have the following,

\displaystyle C_{2k+1}:=\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{v_{2k-1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(k+1)}U_{k}\big)}\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Big(M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}\Big(M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}\big(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\big)}\Big)\\
\hskip 241.84842pt\bigoplus M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)\qquad\,\mbox{if $k$ is odd},\\
\mathrm{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}\big(I_{4}\otimes W^{(k+1)}U_{k}\big)}\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Big(M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k}{2}}}\Big(M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus M_{\widetilde{m}_{k}\big(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\big)}\Big)\Big)\Big)\quad\mbox{if $k$ is even}.\end{cases}

Here,
U_{k}
remains the same unitary obtained in
7.25
.

Proof
:
Consider the unitary
(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}
in
M_{4}^{(k+1)}
. By
7.16
, the set of distinct eigenvalues are either
\{1,\theta^{\,2},\overline{\theta}^{\,2},\ldots,\theta^{\,k-1},\overline{\theta}^{\,k-1},\theta^{\,k+1}\}
or
\{1,\theta^{\,2},\overline{\theta}^{\,2},\ldots,\theta^{\,k},\overline{\theta}^{\,k}\}
depending on
k
being odd or even respectively, and
\sigma\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)=\sigma\big((u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2})^{2}\big)
with cardinality
k+1
. The multiplicities
\widetilde{m}_{k}(.)
of the eigenvalues are given by
7.21
. The rest of the proof follows along the similar line of
7.25
.

\Box

The above lemma can be thought of as a counterpart to
7.26
for the case of
u\sim v
. We have crucially used here the enumeration of eigenvalues according to increasing power of
\theta
followed immediately by
\overline{\theta}
(see also
7.18
and
7.22
) to keep the unitary
U_{k}
same with that in
7.25
. Now we are ready to prove our main theorem.

Theorem 7.32
.

Let
u=u(a)\mbox{ and }v=u(b)
be
4\times 4
Hadamard inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices such that
u\sim v
. If
\theta=\overline{a}b
satisfies
\theta^{\,m}=1
for
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
, then there is a finite index subfactor
R^{(m)}_{u,v}
of the hyperfinite type
II_{1}
factor
R
such that
[R:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]=2m
and
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subseteq R_{u}\cap R_{v}
.

Proof
:
The cases of
m=4,6
are already discussed in detail at the beginning of this subsection. For
\theta^{\,m}=1
with
m\geq 8
, choose
k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m=2k_{m}+2
. Such a positive integer
k_{m}
always exists and
k_{m}\geq 3
. Consider the following inclusion

C_{2k_{m}-1}:=\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k_{m}-1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m})}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{v_{2k_{m}-1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m})}\big)\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m})}=:A_{2k_{m}-1},

which is same as
\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k_{m}-1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k_{m}-2}^{*}v_{2k_{m}-2})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k_{m})}\big)\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m})}
due to
7.10
. The set of eigenvalues of
(u_{2k_{m}-2}^{*}v_{2k_{m}-2})^{2}
is given by
7.16
, and their multiplicities are given by
7.21
. Observe that up to this stage, the eigenvalues, along with their multiplicities, remain the same as in
7.15
and
7.17
for the case of
u\nsim v
. This is because we always take
\theta
to be a primitive even root of unity. Therefore,
7.26
remains valid for
C_{2k_{m}-1}
, and the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{k_{m}-1}
for the inclusion
C_{2k_{m}-1}\subset A_{2k_{m}-1}
is a
(k_{m}+1)\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
, and the square of the norm of it is
||\Lambda_{k_{m}-1}||^{2}=4(k_{m}+1)
(see
7.28
). Now, consider the next inclusion

C_{2k_{m}+1}:=\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k_{m}+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{v_{2k_{m}+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}\big)\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}=:A_{2k_{m}+1},

which is same as
\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k_{m}+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k_{m}}^{*}v_{2k_{m}})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}\big)\subset\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}
due to
7.10
. That is, we are considering the following quadruple

\begin{matrix}A_{2k_{m}-1}&\subset&A_{2k_{m}+1}\\
||&&||\\
\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m})}&&\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k_{m}-1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k_{m}-2}^{*}v_{2k_{m}-2})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k_{m})}\big)&&\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k_{m}+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k_{m}}^{*}v_{2k_{m}})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}\big)\\
||&&||\\
C_{2k_{m}-1}&\subset&C_{2k_{m}+1}\\
\end{matrix}

Recall that if
u
were not related to
v
, then this quadruple is not non-degenerate as explained in
7.29
. This is because in the absence of any such condition on
\theta
, the inclusion matrix for
C_{2k_{m}+1}\subset A_{2k_{m}+1}
is a
(k_{m}+2)\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
by
7.28
. However, we show that putting the condition
\theta^{\,2k_{m}+2}=1
forces a row-drop in the inclusion matrix. More precisely, we claim that the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{k_{m}}
for the right vertical inclusion
C_{2k_{m}+1}\subset A_{2k_{m}+1}
is also a
(k_{m}+1)\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
, and hence
||\Lambda_{k_{m}-1}||^{2}=||\Lambda_{k_{m}}||^{2}=4(k_{m}+1)
. Consider the unitary
(u_{2k_{m}}^{*}v_{2k_{m}})^{2}
in
M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}
. By
7.31
, we see that the number of direct summands in the Wedderburn–Artin decomposition of the algebra
\big((u_{2k_{m}}^{*}v_{2k_{m}})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}
is exactly
k_{m}+1
. Since
A_{2k_{m}+1}=\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}=\oplus M_{4}^{(k_{m}+1)}
, and the unitary
u_{2k+1}(I_{4}\otimes W^{(k+1)}U_{k})
in
7.31
belongs to
A_{2k_{m}+1}
, the inclusion matrix
\Lambda_{k_{m}}
for the inclusion
C_{2k_{m}+1}\subset A_{2k_{m}+1}
becomes a
(k_{m}+1)\times 4
matrix each of whose entry is
1
. This finishes the claim.

Therefore, the following quadruple

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}A_{2k_{m}-1}&\subset&A_{2k_{m}+1}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
C_{2k_{m}-1}&\subset&C_{2k_{m}+1}\\
\end{matrix}

which is already a commuting square, becomes non-degenerate also by Lemma
3.10
in
[
5
]
. Iterating Jones’ basic construction starting with this non-degenerate commuting square, we obtain a subfactor
R^{(m)}_{u,v}
of the hyperfinite type
II_{1}
factor
R
such that
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subseteq R_{u}\cap R_{v}
(by
2.27
) and
[R:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]=||\Lambda_{k_{m}-1}||^{2}=4(k_{m}+1)=2m
. That the tower
A_{1}\subset A_{3}\subset A_{5}\subset\cdots
indeed gives us
R
follows as an application of Theorem
3.5
in
[
1
]
, for instance.

\Box

Therefore, we obtain the following family of finite index subfactors

\left\{R^{(m)}_{u(a),u(b)}\subset R:\,u(a)\sim u(b)\mbox{ with }(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1,\,m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2\right\}

such that
\big[R:R^{(m)}_{u(a),u(b)}\big]=2m
, where
u(a),u(b)
are complex Hadamard matrices of order
4\times 4
parametrized by the circle parameters
a\mbox{ and }b
respectively such that
u(a)\sim u(b)
with
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1
for
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
. The converse of the above result is also true.

Corollary 7.33
.

Given any
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
, there exists a pair
(u,v)
of Hadamard inequivalent
4\times 4
complex Hadamard matrices and a subfactor
R_{0}\subset R
such that
R_{0}\subset R_{u}\cap R_{v}
and
[R:R_{0}]=2m
.

Proof
:
Given any integer
m\geq 4
, choose a complex Hadamard matrix
u(a)
of the required form, where
a
is any arbitrary complex number on the semicircle. Consider
b=\omega a
, where
\omega\neq\pm 1
is any primitive
m
-th root of unity. If
b
lies in the lower semicircle, consider
-b
instead of
b
, which lies in the upper semicircle. Obtain the complex Hadamard matrix
u(b)
. For the pair
(u,v):=(u(a),u(b))
, we have
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1
. By
7.32
, we have the required subfactor
R_{0}:=R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
.

\Box

We shall see in the next subsection that all these subfactors
R^{(m)}_{u(a),u(b)}\subset R
are irreducible. We end this subsection with the following question.

Question:
Can we characterize the following family of finite index subfactors of
R

\left\{R^{(m)}_{u(a),u(b)}\subset R:\,u(a)\sim u(b)\mbox{ with }(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1,\,m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2\right\}?

7.3
Relative commutant and factoriality of
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
when
u\sim v

This subsection is intimately connected with its immediate prequel. Goal of this subsection is to prove that all the subfactors
R^{(m)}_{u(a),u(b)}\subset R
constructed there are irreducible in
R
. As a consequence, we will get that
R_{u(a)}\cap R_{u(b)}
is factor when
u(a)\sim u(b)
. Major tools are the Ocneanu compactness and the spectral decomspoition obtained in subsection
7.2.1
. We begin by recalling the following well-known fact from Linear algebra.

Lemma 7.34
.

Let
B
be a block-diagonal matrix, that is
B\equiv B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
, and
A
be a matrix such that
[A,B]=0
. Suppose that
B_{1}\mbox{ and }B_{2}
do not share any eigenvalue over
\mathbb{C}
. Then,
A
has the same block-diagonal structure as
B
, that is, it is block-diagonal with blocks placed at the same positions as those of
B
.

Proof
:
Let
A=\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}
. Since
[A,B]=0
, we obtain two equations
B_{1}Y-YB_{2}=0
and and
B_{2}Z-ZB_{1}=0
. Since
B_{1}\mbox{ and }B_{2}
do not share any eigenvalue, by the Sylvester criterion, we get that
Y=0
and
Z=0
.

\Box

Lemma 7.35
.

The commutant of any finite-dimensional algebra
N=\oplus_{j=1}^{r}N_{n_{j}}
, where
N_{n_{j}}
is the type
I_{n_{j}}
factor, in the type
I_{m}
factor
M_{m}
such that
\sum_{j}n_{j}=m
is
\mathbb{C}^{\,r}
.

Proof
:
By the previous
7.34
, if
A\in M_{m}
commutes with every
B=\oplus_{j=1}^{r}B_{j}
where
B_{j}\in N_{n_{j}}
, then
A
must be of the form
\oplus_{j=1}^{r}A_{j}
where
A_{j}\in N_{n_{j}}
. This is because it is always possible to choose such a block-diagonal matrix
B
satisfying the hypothesis of
7.34
. Since each
N_{n_{j}}
is the type
I_{n_{j}}
factor, each
A_{j}
must be a scalar matrix of order
n_{j}
.

\Box

Now we prove our main theorem. The crucial ingredients are
7.25
and
7.26
, and more precisely
7.31
. We request the readers to recall the corresponding notations also. Recall that the unitary
U_{k}
in
7.31
is defined inductively by
U_{k}=(I_{4}\otimes U_{k-1})V_{k-1}
, where
V_{k-1}
is certain permutation matrix in
M_{4}^{(k+1)}
that only interchange the diagonal entries (see the proof of
7.25
). This permutation matrix has crucial role in what follows next.

Theorem 7.36
.

Let
u=u(a)\mbox{ and }v=u(b)
be
4\times 4
Hadamard inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices such that
u\sim v
with
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1
. Then, the subfactor
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
is irreducible.

Proof
:
Since
u\sim v
, we have
\theta=\overline{a}b\in\Gamma
. If
\theta^{\,m}=1
(
\theta
is a primitive even root of unity), take
k=(m-2)/2
. The proof is a bit technical, and hence we break it into several steps for clarity. We separate two situations, namely
k
being odd or even. First suppose that
k
is odd. By
7.21
, we have the multiplicities
\widetilde{m}_{k}(.)
of the eigenvalues of
(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}
(and similarly
\widetilde{m}_{k-1}(.)
for
(u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2})^{2}
). To avoid the cumbersome notation, we denote
\widetilde{m}_{k}(.)
simply by
m_{k}(.)
specifically for this proof. We hope that this does not create any confusion because by
7.21
, we see that
\widetilde{m}_{k-1}(.)
is anyway equal to
m_{k-1}(.)
, and so is
\widetilde{m}_{k}(.)
equal to
m_{k}(.)
except for the last eigenvalue
\theta^{\,k+1}
(that is, only
\widetilde{m}_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})\neq m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})
).

Step 1: The action of the permutation matrix

V_{k-1}
(
7.31
,
7.25
).

Consider the unitary matrix
(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}
. Recall from
7.4
that

(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}=p\otimes(u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2})^{2}+q(\theta^{\,2})\otimes(v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2})^{2}

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall from
Equation

7.11
the following,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{W^{(k)}}(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{11}\otimes I_{2}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{W^{(k-1)}}(u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2})^{2}+E_{22}\otimes C(\theta^{\,2})\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{W^{(k-1)}}(v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{11}\otimes I_{2}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{U_{k-1}}\Big(I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\,\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\theta^{\,2r}\,I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\,I_{m_{k-1}\big(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\big)}\Big)\bigoplus\theta^{\,k+1}\,I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)

\displaystyle+E_{22}\otimes C(\theta^{\,2})\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{U_{k-1}}\Big(I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\,\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\,I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\theta^{\,2r}\,I_{m_{k-1}\big(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\big)}\Big)\bigoplus\overline{\theta}^{\,k+1}\,I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{(I_{4}\otimes U_{k-1})V_{k-1}}\Big(I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\theta^{\,2r}\,I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\overline{\theta}^{\,2r}\,I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\Big)\bigoplus\theta^{\,k+1}\,I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\,.

Note that here
\theta^{\,k+1}=\overline{\theta}^{\,k+1}
as
\theta^{\,m}=1
. Since
C(\theta^{\,2})
is the diagonal matrix
\mbox{diag}\{1,\theta^{\,2}\}
, we obtain that under the conjugation action by the permutation matrix
V_{k-1}
, the element

\alpha I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}

where
\alpha,\beta_{2r},\gamma_{2r},\delta\in\mathbb{C}
for
r=1,\ldots,\frac{k-1}{2}
, becomes the following

\displaystyle(E_{11}+E_{22})\otimes\Big\{\alpha I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big\}

\displaystyle+E_{33}\otimes\Big\{\alpha I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big\}

\displaystyle+E_{44}\otimes\Big\{\big(\beta_{2}I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\oplus\alpha I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\big)\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-3}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r+2}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r+2})}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigoplus\big(\delta I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,k-1})}\oplus\gamma_{k-1}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\big)\Big\}\,.

(7.12)

This finishes our first step.

Step 2: The structure of

\,C_{2k+1}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k-1}
.

We find the general structure of this commutant. With the convention
W_{0}=I_{4}
, we have the following,

\displaystyle C_{2k+1}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k-1}

(7.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\Big)\Big\}^{\prime}\cap\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\Big)\Big\}^{\prime}\cap\mbox{Ad}_{I_{4}\otimes u_{2k-1}}\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\Big\{\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\Big)^{\prime}\cap\,\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}^{*}(I_{4}\otimes u_{2k-1})}\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\Big\{\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\Big)^{\prime}\cap\,\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes W_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\Big\{\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{W^{(k+1)}}\Big(\mathrm{Ad}_{U_{k}}\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)\Big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt\bigcap\,\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes W_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)\Big\}\quad\qquad(\mbox{by }\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{mpr})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}(I_{4}\otimes W^{(k+1)})}\Big\{\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{U_{k}}\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\bigcap\,\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes WW_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}\big(I_{4}\otimes W\otimes W^{(k)}U_{k-1}\big)}\Big\{\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\bigcap\,\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes WW_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)\Big\}\,,

where the last line follows from the fact that
U_{s}=(I_{4}\otimes U_{s-1})V_{s-1}
with
U_{s-1}\in M_{4}^{(s)}
. Now, we have

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes WW_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)

(7.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{bl-diag}\Big\{\mbox{Ad}_{W\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\,,\,\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{1}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\,,\,\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{2}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\,,\,\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{3}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\Big\}

where
x\in\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}
. We write
x=\mbox{bl-diag}\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
with
x_{j}\in M_{4}^{(k)}
for
j=1,2,3,4
. Then, using
7.1
it is easy to verify the following four identities,

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{W\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big((E_{11}+E_{22})\otimes(x_{1}+x_{3})+(E_{33}+E_{44})\otimes(x_{2}+x_{4})

\displaystyle\quad+(E_{12}+E_{21})\otimes(x_{1}-x_{3})+(iaE_{34}-i\overline{a}E_{43})\otimes(x_{2}-x_{4})\Big)

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{1}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big((E_{11}+E_{22})\otimes(x_{2}+x_{4})+(E_{33}+E_{44})\otimes(x_{1}+x_{3})

\displaystyle\quad+(E_{12}+E_{21})\otimes(x_{2}-x_{4})+(-i\overline{a}E_{34}+iaE_{43})\otimes(x_{1}-x_{3})\big)

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{2}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big((E_{11}+E_{22})\otimes(x_{1}+x_{3})+(E_{33}+E_{44})\otimes(x_{2}+x_{4})

\displaystyle\quad+(E_{12}+E_{21})\otimes(x_{3}-x_{1})+(-iaE_{34}+i\overline{a}E_{43})\otimes(x_{2}-x_{4})\big)

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{3}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big((E_{11}+E_{22})\otimes(x_{2}+x_{4})+(E_{33}+E_{44})\otimes(x_{1}+x_{3})

(7.15)

\displaystyle\quad+(E_{12}+E_{21})\otimes(x_{4}-x_{2})+(i\overline{a}E_{34}-iaE_{43})\otimes(x_{1}-x_{3})\big)\,.

Section

7.3
describes arbitrary elements of the set in
Equation

7.14
.

Step 3: Proof of

\,C_{2k+1}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k-1}=\mathbb{C}
.

This is the major step in the proof. In view of
Equation

7.13
, it is enough to prove the following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)^{\prime}

(7.16)

\displaystyle\,\bigcap\,\,\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes WW_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)\,.

First observe that since the permutation matrix
V_{k-1}
only interchanges the diagonal elements, we get the following,

\Big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)^{\prime}\cap A_{2k+1}\subseteq\Delta_{4}\otimes\Delta_{4}^{(k+1)}\,.

To see this, apply
2.29
and observe the following,

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\Big(M_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus M_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\mathbb{C}I_{m_{k}(1)}\oplus\mathbb{C}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\mathbb{C}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{C}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\,,

where the last line follows from
7.35
. Therefore, the intersection in the right hand side of
Equation

7.16
must consists of purely diagonal matrices because

\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes WW_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)\subseteq A_{2k+1}:=\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\,.

Since

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{\big(\sum_{j=0}^{3}E_{j+1,j+1}\otimes WW_{j}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\big)}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{bl-diag}\Big\{\mbox{Ad}_{W\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\,,\,\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{1}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\,,\,\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{2}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\,,\,\mbox{Ad}_{WW_{3}^{*}\xi^{*}\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(x)\Big\}

for
x=\mbox{bl-diag}\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}\in\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}
, we see that any element of the intersection in
Equation

7.16
must satisfy
x_{1}=x_{3}
and
x_{2}=x_{4}
, with
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Delta_{4}^{(k)}
because of the identities established in
Section

7.3
in Step
2
. Thus, to prove that the
Equation

7.16
holds, it is enough to prove that the following,

\displaystyle\Big\{\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\alpha I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)\,:\,\alpha,\beta_{2r},\gamma_{2r},\delta\in\mathbb{C}\Big\}

\displaystyle\bigcap\,\Big\{I_{2}\otimes\mbox{bl-diag}\{x_{1},x_{1},x_{2},x_{2},x_{2},x_{2},x_{1},x_{1}\}\,:\,x_{1},x_{2}\in\Delta_{4}^{(k)}\Big\}=\mathbb{C}\,.

(7.17)

Because of the fact that
\Delta_{4}=\Delta_{2}\otimes\Delta_{2}
, to establish
Section

7.3
, it is enough to prove the following,

\displaystyle\Big\{\Delta_{2}\otimes\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\alpha I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)\,:\,\alpha,\beta_{2r},\gamma_{2r},\delta\in\mathbb{C}\Big\}

\displaystyle\bigcap\,\Big\{\mbox{bl-diag}\{x_{1},x_{1},x_{2},x_{2},x_{2},x_{2},x_{1},x_{1}\}\,:\,x_{1},x_{2}\in\Delta_{4}^{(k)}\Big\}=\mathbb{C}\,.

(7.18)

Now, observe that the intersection in
Section

7.3
is determined by the following two intersections,

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\alpha I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)\,:\,\alpha,\beta_{2r},\gamma_{2r},\delta\in\mathbb{C}\Big\}

\displaystyle\bigcap\,\,\mbox{bl-diag}\{x_{1},x_{1},x_{2},x_{2}\}\,,

(7.19)

and

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\alpha^{\prime}I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\gamma^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta^{\prime}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\Big)\,:\,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{2r},\gamma^{\prime}_{2r},\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}\Big\}

\displaystyle\bigcap\,\mbox{bl-diag}\{x_{2},x_{2},x_{1},x_{1}\}\,.

(7.20)

Consider arbitrary element
\xi(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
in
Section

7.3

\xi(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta):=\mathrm{Ad}_{V_{k-1}}\Big(\alpha I_{m_{k}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,2r})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\Big)\,,

and similarly
\xi(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
in
Section

7.3
. Then
\xi(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
(similarly
\xi(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
) is of the form described in
Section

7.3
in Step
1
. Therefore, we get the following six equations,

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\gamma^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\beta^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta^{\prime}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\beta^{\prime}_{2}I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\oplus\alpha^{\prime}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\big)\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-3}{2}}\Big(\beta^{\prime}_{2r+2}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\oplus\gamma^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r+2})}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\bigoplus\big(\delta^{\prime}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\oplus\gamma^{\prime}_{k-1}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\big)\,,

and

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\beta_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\beta_{2}I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\oplus\alpha I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\big)\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-3}{2}}\Big(\beta_{2r+2}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2r})}\oplus\gamma_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2r+2})}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 125.19194pt\bigoplus\big(\delta I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\oplus\gamma_{k-1}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\big)

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}I_{m_{k-1}(1)}\bigoplus\bigoplus_{r=1}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\Big(\beta^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,2})}\oplus\gamma^{\prime}_{2r}I_{m_{k-1}(\overline{\theta}^{\,2})}\Big)\bigoplus\delta^{\prime}I_{m_{k-1}(\theta^{\,k+1})}\,.

If the associated ‘matrix order’ with each complex number is understood and creates no confusion, writing in terms of tuple, these six equations are the following set of equalities,

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta_{2},\gamma_{2},\beta_{4},\gamma_{4},\ldots,\beta_{k-1},\gamma_{k-1},\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}_{4},\beta^{\prime}_{4},\ldots,\gamma^{\prime}_{k-1},\beta^{\prime}_{k-1},\delta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta_{2}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{4},\gamma^{\prime}_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{6},\gamma^{\prime}_{4},\ldots,\beta^{\prime}_{k-1},\gamma^{\prime}_{k-3},\delta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}_{k-1})\,,

and

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha,\gamma_{2},\beta_{2},\gamma_{4},\beta_{4},\ldots,\gamma_{k-1},\beta_{k-1},\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta_{2},\alpha,\beta_{4},\gamma_{2},\beta_{6},\gamma_{4},\ldots,\beta_{k-1},\gamma_{k-3},\delta,\gamma_{k-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}_{2},\beta^{\prime}_{4},\gamma^{\prime}_{4},\ldots,\beta^{\prime}_{k-1},\gamma^{\prime}_{k-1},\delta^{\prime})\,.

Immediately we see that
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
and
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
. From the second and third equations associated with
x_{1}
, we get the following,

\alpha^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}_{2r}=\beta^{\prime}_{2r+2}=\delta^{\prime}\,\,\mbox{ and }\,\,\alpha^{\prime}=\gamma_{2r}^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}_{2r+2}=\delta^{\prime}

for
1\leq r\leq\frac{k-3}{2}
. These two equations together with
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
and
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
shows that
x_{1}=\alpha(1,\ldots,1)
. Moreover, by the third equation associated with
x_{2}
, we see that
x_{2}=\alpha(1,\ldots,1)
. Hence, we finally get that
x_{1}=x_{2}=\alpha(1,\ldots,1)
, that is,
\xi(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)=\xi(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})=\alpha 1
. This finally establishes the claim in
Equation

7.16
, and finishes this step.

Step 4: The Ocneanu compactness.

This is the final step of the proof. First, briefly recall the construction of
R^{(m)}_{u,v}
from
7.32
. The following quadruple

\begin{matrix}A_{2k-1}&\subset&A_{2k+1}\\
||&&||\\
\mathbb{C}\otimes\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}&&\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k-1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k)}\big)&&\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k+1}}\big(\mathbb{C}\otimes\big((u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)\\
||&&||\\
C_{2k-1}&\subset&C_{2k+1}\end{matrix}

becomes a non-degenerate commuting square, and we have started iterating the basic construction from this stage. If
D_{2k+j}
, for
j\geq 3
odd integer, denote the finite-dimensional subalgebras of
C_{2k+j}
obtained through the basic construction, then
R^{(m)}_{u,v}
is constructed as the SOT-limit of
D_{2k+j}
, whereas
R
is the SOT-limit of
A_{2k+j}
. Note that we have used the convention that
D_{2k+i}=C_{2k+i}
for
i=\pm 1
. Since for
j\geq-1
odd integer, we have
(D_{2k+j}\subset A_{2k+j},D_{2k+j+2}\subset A_{2k+j+2})
is a tower of symmetric commuting squares with limit
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
, by the Ocneanu compactness we have the following,

\big(R^{(m)}_{u,v}\big)^{\,\prime}\cap R=D_{2k+1}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k-1}=C_{2k+1}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k-1}\,.

Now, by the Step
3
we get the irreducibility of
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
when
k
is odd.

Finally, the exact similar analysis holds when
k
is even in Step
1
, which completes the proof.

\Box

Theorem 7.37
.

For the pair of spin model subfactors
R_{u(a)},R_{u(b)}\subset R
such that
u(a)\sim u(b)
, we have
R_{u(a)}\cap R_{u(b)}
is a factor and
R_{u(a)}\cap R_{u(b)}=R^{(m)}_{u(a),u(b)}
if
\,(\overline{a}b)^{\,m}=1
.

Proof
:
Let
u=u(a)\mbox{ and }v=u(b)
be
4\times 4
Hadamard inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices such that
u\sim v
. If
\theta=\overline{a}b
satisfies
\,\theta^{\,m}=1
, we take
k=(m-2)/2
. By
7.36
, we immediately see that
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is a factor since
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R_{u}\cap R_{v}
and
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
is irreducible. For the identification of
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
with
R^{(m)}_{u,v}
, it is enough to prove that
[R_{u}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=[R_{u}:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]
. Note that
[R_{u}:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]=\frac{m}{2}
.

We claim that
[R_{u}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=\lambda^{-1}(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})=\frac{m}{2}
. The first equality is obvious by
[
36
]
, since
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is a factor. To prove the second equality, recall that
R_{u}
is the SOT-limit of
B^{u}_{2j+1}
and
R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is that of
C_{2j+1}
. Given
\theta^{\,m}=1
, if we take
k=(m-2)/2
, then
\sigma\big((u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2})^{2}\big)=\sigma\big((u_{2k+2j}^{*}v_{2k+2j})^{2}\big)
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and cardinality of the spectrum is
k+1
(see
7.16
). Since
(u_{2k+2j}^{*}v_{2k+2j})^{2}
is diagonalizable for all
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}
, the sum of the multiplicities of the eigenvalues of
(u_{2k+2j}^{*}v_{2k+2j})^{2}
will add up to the order of
M_{4}^{(k+j+1)}
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}
, which is equal to
4^{k+j+1}
. Note that the eigenvalues remain the same, but the multiplicities are changing with
j
. Since
C_{2k+2j+1}
is given by the algebra
\big((u_{2k+2j}^{*}v_{2k+2j})^{2}\big)^{\prime}\cap M_{4}^{(k+j+1)}
(
7.10
), we see that at each stage of the inclusion
C_{2k+2j+1}\subset B^{u}_{2k+2j+1}
, we have
(k+1)
-many Wedderburn-Artin direct summands in
M_{4^{k+j+1}}
, and order of the summands add up to
4^{k+j+1}
.

Now, each quadruple
(C_{2r+1}\subset B^{u}_{2r+1},C_{2r+3}\subset B^{u}_{2r+3})
is a commuting square for
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, which follows as an application of
3.2
. By
2.26
and
2.13
, we see that
\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})
is the limit of the eventually constant sequence
\{(k+1)^{-1}\}
. Therefore,
\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})^{-1}=k+1=\frac{m}{2}
, which proves the claim. Thus, we get that
\lambda(R_{u},R_{u}\cap R_{v})^{-1}=[R_{u}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=\frac{m}{2}=[R_{u}:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]
. By the multiplicativity of the Jones’ index, we immediately get that
R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}
.

\Box

We remark that using the above theorem, it takes only a little more effort to show that in the following tower of finite-dimensional algebras

C_{1}\subset C_{3}\subset C_{5}\subset\ldots\subset C_{2k-1}\subset C_{2k+1}\subset\ldots\subset R_{u}\cap R_{v}

if
\theta^{\,m}=1
, then
C_{2k-1}\subset C_{2k+1}\subset\ldots\subset R_{u}\cap R_{v}
is a tower of basic construction, where
k=(m-2)/2
. To see this, fix a
m
and let
k=(m-2)/2
. Recall that the subfactor
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
is the limit of the tower of symmetric commuting squares
(D_{2k+j}\subset A_{2k+j},D_{2k+j+2}\subset A_{2k+j+2})
, for
j\geq-1
odd integer, where
D_{2k+j}=C_{2k+j}
for
j=\pm 1
and
D_{2k+j}\subset C_{2k+j}
for
j\geq 3
. Since for
j\geq 3,\,(D_{2k+j}\subset A_{2k+j},D_{2k+j+2}\subset A_{2k+j+2})
and
(C_{2k+j}\subset A_{2k+j},C_{2k+j+2}\subset A_{2k+j+2})
both are commuting squares in the following diagram

\begin{matrix}\subset&A_{2k+3}&\subset&A_{2k+5}&\subset&A_{2k+7}&\subset&\cdots&\subset&R\\
&\cup&&\cup&&\cup&&&&\cup\\
\subset&C_{2k+3}&\subset&C_{2k+5}&\subset&C_{2k+7}&\subset&\cdots&\subset&R_{u}\cap R_{v}\\
&||&&\cup&&\cup&&&&\cup\\
&D_{2k+3}&\subset&D_{2k+5}&\subset&D_{2k+7}&\subset&\cdots&\subset&R^{(m)}_{u,v}\\
\end{matrix}

we have that
(D_{2k+j}\subset C_{2k+j},D_{2k+j+2}\subset C_{2k+j+2})
is also a commuting square for any
j\geq 3
odd integer. This follows from the following,

E_{D_{2k+j+2}}^{C_{2k+j+2}}(C_{2k+j})=E_{D_{2k+j+2}}^{B^{u}_{2k+j+2}}(C_{2k+j})\subset E_{D_{2k+j+2}}^{B^{u}_{2k+j+2}}(B^{u}_{2k+j})\subset D_{2k+j}\,.

Now, by Proposition
2.6
in
[
36
]
and
7.37
, we get that
1=\lambda(R_{u}\cap R_{v},R^{(m)}_{u,v})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\lambda(C_{2k+j},D_{2k+j})
decreasingly. Since
\lambda
is always less than or equal to
1
, we get that
\lambda(C_{2k+j},D_{2k+j})=1
for all
j\geq 3
odd integer. This says that
C_{2k+j}\subseteq D_{2k+j}
for all
j
by
2.12
, and since the reverse inclusion is already there, we finally get that
C_{2k+j}=D_{2k+j}
for all
j\geq 3
odd integer. Since the tower
D_{2k+3}\subset D_{2k+7}\subset\cdots
is the iterated basic construction for the inclusion
C_{2k-1}\subset C_{2k+1}
, our claim follows.

Corollary 7.38
.

For
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, if
\big(\overline{a}b\big)^{m}=\big(\overline{c}d\big)^{n}=1
and
m\neq n
, then the corresponding subfactors
R_{u(a)}\cap R_{u(b)}\subset R
and
R_{u(c)}\cap R_{u(d)}\subset R
are non-isomorphic.

Corollary 7.39
.

For Hadamard inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices
u\mbox{ and }v
such that
u\sim v
, the quadruple of
II_{1}
factors
(R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u},R_{v}\subset R)
is obtained as an iterated basic construction of a non-degenerate commuting cube.

Proof
:
Follows from
7.32
and
7.37
, and the discussion in
Section

3
.

7.3.1
The interior, exterior angles and its rigidity

We show that the (interior) angle between
R_{u}
and
R_{v}
in the sense of
[
2
]
(see
Section

2.1
) has certain rigidity when
u\sim v
. Recall from
7.37
that if
u\sim v
and
\theta^{\,m}=1
for some
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
, where
u=u(a)
and
v=u(b)
with
\theta=\overline{a}b
, then we have
R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}
. Therefore, we get a family of quadruples of
II_{1}
factors

\begin{matrix}R_{u}&\subset&R\cr\cup&&\cup\cr R_{u}\cap R_{v}&\subset&R_{v}&\end{matrix}

such that
[R:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=2m
and
[R_{u}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=[R_{v}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=m/2
. Recall that given any quadruple of finite index subfactors

\begin{matrix}P&\subset&M\cr\cup&&\cup\cr N&\subset&Q&\end{matrix}

the interior angle
\alpha^{N}_{M}(P,Q)
and the exterior angle
\beta^{N}_{M}(P,Q)
have the following formulae (see
[
2
]
):

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{M}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{[M:N]\,tr(e_{P}e_{Q})-1}{\sqrt{[P:N]-1}\sqrt{[Q:N]-1}}\,,

\displaystyle\beta^{N}_{M}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{tr(e_{P}e_{Q})-tr(e_{P})tr(e_{Q})}{\sqrt{tr(e_{P})-{tr(e_{P})}^{2}}\sqrt{tr(e_{Q})-{tr(e_{Q})}^{2}}}\,.

We also know that
\lambda(P,Q)=\displaystyle\frac{tr(e_{P}e_{Q})}{tr(e_{P})}
if
N\subset M
is irreducible (Theorem
3.3
in
[
4
]
). Since in our case,
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
is irreducible by
7.36
, and
\lambda(R_{u},R_{v})=1/2
by
7.8
, we have
tr(e_{R_{u}}e_{R_{v}})=1/8
. Thus, by the above formulae of angles we see that
\cos\beta(R_{u},R_{v})=1/3
and
\cos\alpha(R_{u},R_{v})=\frac{m-4}{2(m-2)}<1/2
. Therefore, we conclude that
\alpha(R_{u},R_{v})>\pi/3.
We summarize these findings as the following theorem.

Theorem 7.40
.

The interior angle
\alpha\big(R_{u},R_{v}\big)
is strictly greater than
\pi/3
and the exterior angle
\beta\big(R_{u},R_{v}\big)=\arccos 1/3
. Furthermore,
\alpha\big(R_{u},R_{v}\big)
converges to
\pi/3
decreasingly as
m
tends to infinity.

As an immediate corollary, using
2.6
we obtain the following.

Corollary 7.41
.

For any
4\times 4
Hadamard inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices
u\mbox{ and }v
such that
u\sim v
, the following quadruple of subfactors

\begin{matrix}R_{u}&\subset&R\cr\cup&&\cup\cr R_{u}\cap R_{v}&\subset&R_{v}&\end{matrix}

is a commuting square if and only if
m=4
, that is,
b=\pm ia
. Moreover, the above quadruple is never a co-commuting square.

7.4
The Sano-Watatani angle and Connes-Størmer relative entropy

For inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices
u\mbox{ and }v
, we compute the exact value of relative entropy
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
(along with its variant
h(R_{u}|R_{v})
) and the Sano-Watatani angle
\mathrm{Ang}_{R}(R_{u},R_{v})
for the case of
u\sim v
. When
u\nsim v
, we give a legitimate upper and lower bounds for
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
. As a crucial tool, we have shown the existence of a proper subfactor of
R
containing both
R_{u}
and
R_{v}
.

7.4.1
The subfactor
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R

This subsection is devoted to prove the existence of an index
2
subfactor of
R
containing both
R_{u}\mbox{ and }R_{v}
. Recall that we have pair of irreducible subfactors
R_{u}
and
R_{v}
of
R
each with index
4
. Consider the von Neumann subalgebra
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R
. By the irreducibility of
R_{u}\subset R
we get that
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R
is a factor. We show that
R_{u}\vee R_{v}
is a proper subfactor
R
. We begin with the following notations which are at par with that in the beginning of
Section

7
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, let

\displaystyle A_{2k}

\displaystyle=M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}\,,

\displaystyle B^{u}_{2k}

\displaystyle=u_{2k}(\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)})u_{2k}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle B^{v}_{2k}

\displaystyle=v_{2k}(\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)})v_{2k}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2k}

\displaystyle=B^{u}_{2k}\vee B^{v}_{2k}\,.

Note that these are the even steps in the tower of basic constructions described in
Figure

11
.

Lemma 7.42
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\},\,\mathcal{I}_{2k}
is a proper subalgebra of
A_{2k}
.

Proof
:
Since each
A_{2k}
is factor, it is enough to show that
\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{2k}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. First observe that

\displaystyle(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap A_{2k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}\big((\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)})^{\prime}\cap\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}^{*}}(A_{2k})\big)

(7.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}\big((\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)})^{\prime}\cap(M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)})\big)\qquad\big(\mbox{as }u_{2k}\in A_{2k}=M_{4}^{(k+1)}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})\,,

and similarly,
(B^{v}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap A_{2k}=\mbox{Ad}_{v_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})
. Now,
\mathcal{I}_{2k}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k}=\{B^{u}_{2k}\cup B^{v}_{2k}\}^{\prime}\cap A_{2k}=(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap A_{2k}
. Hence, by
Equation

7.21
we need to show the following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})\cap\mbox{Ad}_{v_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}),

(7.22)

or in other words,

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}\big((\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})\cap\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})\big).

(7.23)

Recall from
7.4
that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, one has the following identity in
M_{4}^{(k+1)}

u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}=p\otimes u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2}+q(\theta)\otimes v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2}\,,

with the convention
u_{0}=u\mbox{ and }v_{0}=v
. Applying
7.12
to this identity, we get the following,

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}(u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k})

(7.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{bl-diag}\{u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2}\,,\,u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2}\,,\,v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2}\,,\,\theta\,v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2}\}\,,

where
W
is the self-adjoint unitary defined in
Equation

7.6
. Now, we have the following identity

\displaystyle(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})\cap\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})

(7.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}\Big(\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}}\big(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}\Big(\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}}\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}}\Big(\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})}\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\Big)\,.

Therefore, to prove
Equation

7.23
, it is enough to show the following inclusion

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}\subseteq\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})}\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)

(7.26)

in view of
Equation

7.25
. Consider any arbitrary
(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}
and the diagonal matrix
D=\mbox{diag}\{z,w,z,w\}\in\Delta_{4}
. It is a straightforward verification that
WDW=D
(recall
W
from
Equation

7.6
). Hence,
D\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\in\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}
. Now by
Equation

7.24
, we have the following,

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})}(D\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Ad}_{\mbox{bl-diag}\{u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2}\,,\,u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2}\,,\,v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2}\,,\,\theta\,v_{2k-2}^{*}u_{2k-2}\}}(D\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}\,,

since
u_{2k-2}^{*}v_{2k-2}
is a unitary and
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. This shows that the matrix
D\otimes I_{4}^{(k)}
, where
D=\mbox{diag}\{z,w,z,w\}
for
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
, lies in the intersection

\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)\cap\mbox{Ad}_{(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})u_{2k}^{*}v_{2k}(W\otimes I_{4}^{(k)})}\big(\mbox{Ad}_{W}(\Delta_{4})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)}\big)

which validates
Equation

7.26
. Therefore,
Equation

7.23
holds, and consequently we have

\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})\cap\mbox{Ad}_{v_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(k)})=\mathcal{I}_{2k}^{\,\prime}\cap A_{2k}\,,

by
Equations

7.21
and
7.22
, which concludes the proof.

\Box

Lemma 7.43
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, both
B^{u}_{2k}\mbox{ and }B^{v}_{2k}
are proper subalgebras of
\,\mathcal{I}_{2k}
.

Proof
:
On contrary assume that there exists
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{2k_{0}}=B^{u}_{2k_{0}}
(or
v
in place of
u
). Then
B^{v}_{2k_{0}}\subseteq B^{u}_{2k_{0}}
, and consequently
B^{v}_{2k}\subseteq B^{u}_{2k}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
(recall the tower of basic constructions descibed in
Figure

11
). Thus, we get that
R_{v}\subset R_{u}
. Since
[R:R_{u}]=[R:R_{v}]=4
, we get that
R_{u}=R_{v}
, and consequently
\lambda(R_{u},R_{v})=1
. However, this is a contradiction to
7.8
, and completes the proof.

\Box

Theorem 7.44
.

Let
u
and
v
be (distinct)
4\times 4
inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices. Consider the pair of spin model subfactors
R_{u},R_{v}\subset R
. Then,
R_{u}\vee R_{v}
is a proper subfactor of
R
and
[R:R_{u}\vee R_{v}]=2
.

Proof
:
Given
u\mbox{ and }v
, consider the von Neumann subalgebra
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R
. Note that
R_{u}\vee R_{v}=\big(\cup_{k}\mathcal{I}_{2k}\big)^{\prime\prime}\subset R
, where
\mathcal{I}_{2k}=B^{u}_{2k}\vee B^{v}_{2k}
. By the irreducibility of the spin model subfactor
R_{u}\subset R
, we get the factoriality of
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R
. Hence, the only nontrivial part is to show that
[R:R_{u}\vee R_{v}]=2
. We appeal
7.42
,
7.43
and
3.1
to prove this.

Step 1 :
For each
k
, the quadruple

\begin{matrix}B^{u}_{2k+2}\vee B^{v}_{2k+2}&\subset&A_{2k+2}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
B^{u}_{2k}\vee B^{v}_{2k}&\subset&A_{2k}\\
\end{matrix}

is a co-commuting square.

We need to show that the following quadruple

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}A_{2k}^{\prime}&\subset&(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k})^{\prime}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
A_{2k+2}^{\prime}&\subset&(B^{u}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\\
\end{matrix}

(7.27)

is a commuting square for each
k
. Fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that the quadruple

\begin{matrix}B^{u}_{2k+2}&\subset&A_{2k+2}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
B^{u}_{2k}&\subset&A_{2k}\\
\end{matrix}

is a co-commuting square by the construction of
R_{u}\subset R
. Hence, the quadruple

\begin{matrix}A_{2k}^{\prime}&\subset&(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
A_{2k+2}^{\prime}&\subset&(B^{u}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\\
\end{matrix}

is a commuting square. Now, in
Equation

7.27
we have the following,

\displaystyle E^{(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k})^{\prime}}_{A_{2k}^{\prime}}\big((B^{u}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k})^{\prime}}_{{A_{2k}}^{\prime}}E^{(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}}_{(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k})^{\prime}}\big((B^{u}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}}_{A_{2k}^{\prime}}\big((B^{u}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\cap(B^{v}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle E^{(B^{u}_{2k})^{\prime}}_{A_{2k}^{\prime}}\big((B^{u}_{2k+2})^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle A_{2k+2}^{\prime}\,,

which completes Step
1
.

Step 2 :

||\Lambda_{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}^{A_{2k}}||=||\Lambda^{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||=\sqrt{2}
for all
k
.

Since
B^{u}_{2k}=\mbox{Ad}_{u_{2k}}(\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)})
, we have
||\Lambda^{A_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||=||\Lambda^{M_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}}_{\Delta_{4}\otimes M_{4}^{(k)}}||=2
. By the multiplicativity of the Watatani index, we have the equality
||\Lambda^{A_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||^{2}=||\Lambda^{A_{2k}}_{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}||^{2}\,||\Lambda^{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||^{2}
. Therefore, we get that
2=||\Lambda^{A_{2k}}_{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}||\,||\Lambda^{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||
. By
7.42
and
7.43
, we have
||\Lambda^{A_{2k}}_{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}||>1
and
||\Lambda^{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||>1
respectively. Hence by Theorem
1.1.1
in
[
15
]
, both
||\Lambda^{A_{2k}}_{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}||
and
||\Lambda^{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||
are greater than equal to
\sqrt{2}
. Thus, we get that
||\Lambda_{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}^{A_{2k}}||=||\Lambda^{\mathcal{I}_{2k}}_{B^{u}_{2k}}||=\sqrt{2}
, which finishes this step.

Finally, applying
3.1
together with Step
1
and Step
2
, we obtain that the following quadruple

\begin{matrix}B^{u}_{2k+2}\vee B^{v}_{2k+2}&\subset&A_{2k+2}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
B^{u}_{2k}\vee B^{v}_{2k}&\subset&A_{2k}\\
\end{matrix}

is a symmetric commuting square for all
k
. Since
R=\overline{\cup_{k}A_{2k}}^{\,\mbox{sot}}
and
R_{u}\vee R_{v}=\big(\cup_{k}\mathcal{I}_{2k}\big)^{\prime\prime}\subset R
, we get that the following quadruple

\begin{matrix}R_{u}\vee R_{v}&\subset&R\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mathcal{I}_{0}=B^{u}_{0}\vee B^{v}_{0}&\subset&A_{0}\\
\end{matrix}

is a symmetric commuting square. Therefore,
[R:R_{u}\vee R_{v}]=||\Lambda^{A_{0}}_{\mathcal{I}_{0}}||^{2}=2
by Step
2
.

\Box

7.4.2
The Sano-Watatani angle when
u\sim v

Let us begin at the beginning. We first compute the angle between
\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\subset M_{4}
and
\mbox{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})\subset M_{4}
, and show that the following quadruple

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})&\subset&M_{4}\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\cap\mbox{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})&\subset&\mbox{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})\\
\end{matrix}

(7.28)

is not always a commuting square. This is another example where the floor (and consequently the roof due to
3.5
) in
Figure

3
need not be commuting square.

Proposition 7.45
.

If
u\mbox{ and }v
are parametrized by the circle parameters
a\mbox{ and }b
respectively, then the cosine of the angle between
\mathrm{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\mbox{ and }\mathrm{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})
is the set
\{0,|\Re(\overline{a}b)|\}
, and the quadruple in
Equation

7.28
is a commuting square if and only if
b=\pm ia
.

Proof
:
Consider the nonnegative operator
E_{u\Delta_{4}u^{*}}E_{v\Delta_{4}v^{*}}E_{u\Delta_{4}u^{*}}
, and observe that as an element of
\mathcal{B}(M_{4},tr)=M_{16}
, this is same as the following operator

\mbox{Ad}_{u}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}}\mbox{Ad}_{v}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\mbox{Ad}_{v^{*}}\mbox{Ad}_{u}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}}=\mbox{Ad}_{u}\circ E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}v}(\Delta_{4})}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\circ\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}}.

First consider the operator
E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}v}(\Delta_{4})}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\in\mathcal{B}(M_{4})
. Let
\,\theta=\overline{a}b
and
\{E_{ij}:1\leq i,j\leq 4\}
be the standard set of matrix units in
M_{4}
. Then, observe the following,

\displaystyle E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{u^{*}v\Delta_{4}v^{*}u}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}(E_{ij})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{u^{*}v\Delta_{4}v^{*}u}(\delta_{ij}E_{ij})

(7.29)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}v}\,E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\left(v^{*}uE_{ii}u^{*}v\right)\,.

It is easy to verify that
E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\left(v^{*}uE_{ii}u^{*}v\right)=E_{ii}
for
i=1,3
, and

\displaystyle E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\left(v^{*}uE_{22}u^{*}v\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{diag}\{0,(2+\theta+\overline{\theta})/4,0,(2-\theta-\overline{\theta})/4\}\,,

\displaystyle E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\left(v^{*}uE_{44}u^{*}v\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{diag}\{0,(2-\theta-\overline{\theta})/4,0,(2+\theta+\overline{\theta})/4\}\,.

Therefore, we have the following,

\displaystyle E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}\big(\mbox{Ad}_{v^{*}u}(E_{ii})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{i1}E_{11}+\delta_{i3}E_{33}+\delta_{i2}\left(\frac{2+\theta+\overline{\theta}}{4}E_{22}+\frac{2-\theta-\overline{\theta}}{4}E_{44}\right)+\delta_{i4}\left(\frac{2-\theta-\overline{\theta}}{4}E_{22}+\frac{2+\theta+\overline{\theta}}{4}E_{44}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{i1}E_{11}+\delta_{i3}E_{33}+\delta_{i2}\left(\frac{2+t}{4}E_{22}+\frac{2-t}{4}E_{44}\right)+\delta_{i4}\left(\frac{2-t}{4}E_{22}+\frac{2+t}{4}E_{44}\right)\,,

where
t=\theta+\overline{\theta}=\overline{a}b+a\overline{b}\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, by
Equation

7.29
we finally get the following

\displaystyle E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{u^{*}v\Delta_{4}v^{*}u}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}(E_{ij})

(7.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\delta_{i1}E_{11}+\delta_{i3}E_{33}+\frac{1}{8}\big((4+t^{2})\delta_{i2}+(4-t^{2})\delta_{i4}\big)E_{22}\\
+\frac{1}{8}\big((4-t^{2})\delta_{i2}+(4+t^{2})\delta_{i4}\big)E_{44}&\mbox{ if }\,\,i=j,\cr 0&\mbox{ if }\,\,i\neq j.\cr\end{cases}

Now fix the ordered orthornormal basis
\mathfrak{B}:=\{2E_{11},2E_{22},2E_{33},2E_{44},2E_{k\ell}\,:\,1\leq k,\ell\leq 4\,,\,k\neq\ell\}
of
M_{4}
. By
Equation

7.30
we get that the matrix of the linear operator
E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{u^{*}v\Delta_{4}v^{*}u}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}
acting on
M_{4}
, when viewed as a linear map from
\mathbb{C}^{16}
to
\mathbb{C}^{16}
, with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}
is a
16\times 16
matrix having zero everywhere else except the following matrix

B:=\left[{\begin{matrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{8}(4+t^{2})&0&\frac{1}{8}(4-t^{2})\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&\frac{1}{8}(4-t^{2})&0&\frac{1}{8}(4+t^{2})\\
\end{matrix}}\right]

at the top left
4\times 4
block.

Now, recall from
7.9
that
\mathrm{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\cap\mathrm{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})=\mathrm{Ad}_{u}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
, where the emebedding
\mathbb{C}^{3}\xhookrightarrow{}\Delta_{4}\subseteq M_{4}
is given by
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\mapsto(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2})
. The trace preserving conditional expectation
E^{M_{4}}_{\mathrm{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\,\cap\,\mathrm{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})}
is given by
\mbox{Ad}_{u}\circ E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}\,\cap\,\mathrm{Ad}_{u^{*}v}(\Delta_{4})}\circ\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}}=\mbox{Ad}_{u}\circ E^{M_{4}}_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}\circ\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}}
, where
E^{M_{4}}_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}:(a_{ij})\longmapsto\mbox{diag}\big\{a_{11},\frac{1}{2}(a_{22}+a_{44}),a_{33},\frac{1}{2}(a_{22}+a_{44})\big\}
. Hence, the matrix of the linear operator
E^{M_{4}}_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}
acting on
M_{4}
, when viewed as a linear map from
\mathbb{C}^{16}
to
\mathbb{C}^{16}
, with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}
is a
16\times 16
matrix having zero everywhere else except the following matrix

\widetilde{B}:=\left[{\begin{matrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
\end{matrix}}\right]

at the top left
4\times 4
block.

Therefore, we see that the quadruple in
Equation

7.28
is a commuting square if and only if the matrix
B-\widetilde{B}=0
. This happens if and only if
t=0
, and consequently
b=\pm ia
.

Now, suppose that this condition is not satisfied. The spectrum of the following positive operator

E^{M_{4}}_{\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})}E^{M_{4}}_{\mbox{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})}E^{M_{4}}_{\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})}-E^{M_{4}}_{\mathrm{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\,\cap\,\mathrm{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})}

acting on
M_{4}
, which is same as the spectrum of the operator
E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}E^{M_{4}}_{\mbox{Ad}_{u^{*}v}(\Delta_{4})}E^{M_{4}}_{\Delta_{4}}-E^{M_{4}}_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}
, simply becomes the spectrum of the
4\times 4
matrix
B-\widetilde{B}
. This is because
\mbox{Ad}_{u}\in\mathcal{B}(M_{4})
is a unitary. Finally, observe that the spectrum of
B-\widetilde{B}
is the union of
\{0\}
and the spectrum of the
2\times 2
matrix
\frac{t^{2}}{8}(I_{2}-\sigma_{1})
, which is
\{0,t^{2}/4\}
. Therefore, the cosine of the angle between
\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\mbox{ and }\mbox{Ad}_{v}(\Delta_{4})
is the set
\{0,|t|/2\}
. Since
t=\overline{a}b+a\overline{b}
, the proof is completed.

\Box

Therefore, the quadruple
(\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\mathbb{C}^{3})\subset\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4}),\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\Delta_{4})\subset M_{4})
in
Equation

7.28
is not a commuting square when
b\neq\pm ia
. Using this, and the techniques of commuting cube introduced in
Section

3
, we now obtain a complete characterization for the quadruple of von Neumann algebras
(R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u},R_{v}\subset R)
being a commuting square. More precisely, we prove the following.

Theorem 7.46
.

(Characterization of commuting square) Given two
4\times 4
Hadamard inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices
u(a)
and
u(b)
parametrized by the circle parameters
a\mbox{ and }b
, the quadruple of von Neumann algebras

\begin{matrix}R_{u(b)}&\subset&R\\
\cup&&\cup\\
R_{u(a)}\cap R_{u(b)}&\subset&R_{u(a)}\\
\end{matrix}

is a commuting square if and only if
(\overline{a}b)^{4}=1
, that is
b=\pm ia
.

Proof
:
We have the following commuting squares

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}R_{u}&\subset&R\\
\cup&&\cup\\
u\Delta_{4}u^{*}&\subset&M_{4}\\
\end{matrix}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\begin{matrix}R_{v}&\subset&R\\
\cup&&\cup\\
v\Delta_{4}v^{*}&\subset&M_{4}\\
\end{matrix}\,.

(7.31)

by the construction of
R_{u},R_{v}\subset R
. Recall from
7.9
that
u\Delta_{4}u^{*}\cap v\Delta_{4}v^{*}=\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
. Obtain the cube as described in
Figure

15
,

Figure 15:
Commuting cube in
4\times 4

where the commuting squares in
Equation

7.31
are the adjacent faces. By
3.2
, the slice

\begin{matrix}R_{u}\cap R_{v}&\subset&R\\
\cup&&\cup\\
\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\mathbb{C}^{3})&\subset&M_{4}\\
\end{matrix}

is a commuting square. Hence, by
3.5
we get that if the roof
(R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u},R_{v}\subset R)
is a commuting square, then the floor
(\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\mathbb{C}^{3})\subset u\Delta_{4}u^{*},v\Delta_{4}v^{*}\subset M_{4})
must be also so. By
7.45
, the quadruple
(\mbox{Ad}_{u}(\mathbb{C}^{3})\subset u\Delta_{4}u^{*},v\Delta_{4}v^{*}\subset M_{4})
is not a commuting square when
b\neq\pm ia
(equivalently
(\overline{a}b)^{4}=1
), as there is a non-trivial angle between them. Therefore, when
b\neq\pm ia
the quadruple
(R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u},R_{v}\subset R)
is not a commuting square, or in other words, if the quadruple
(R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u},R_{v}\subset R)
is a commuting square, then the condition
b=\pm ia
is necessary. By
7.41
, we see that this is also a sufficient condition, which completes the proof.

\Box

Theorem 7.47
.

Let
u=u(a)
and
v=u(b)
be distinct
4\times 4
inequivalent complex Hadamard matrices parametrized by the circle parameters
a\mbox{ and }b
such that
u\sim v
with
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1,\,m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
. Then, we have the following.

(i)

If
\,m=4
, then
\mathrm{Ang}_{R}(R_{u},R_{v})=\{\pi/2\};

(ii)

If
\,m\geq 6
, then
\mathrm{Ang}_{R}(R_{u},R_{v})=\left\{\frac{2k\pi}{m}\,:\,k=1,2,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{m-2}{4}\rfloor\right\}
.

Proof
:
Part
(i)
is an immediate consequence of
7.46
. For part
(ii)
, first observe that
\text{Ang}_{R}(R_{u},R_{v})=\text{Ang}_{R_{u}\vee R_{v}}(R_{u},R_{v})
(see Lemma
3.1
in
[
42
]
). The rest follows from
7.44
together with Theorem
6.1
in
[
42
]
(putting
n=m/2
), since
[R_{u}\vee R_{v}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=m
by Theorem (
7.32
,
7.37
).

\Box

Remark 7.48
.

Although the subfactor
R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R
becomes a composition of group-type subfactors
(R_{u}\vee R_{v})^{D_{n}}\subset R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset(R_{u}\vee R_{v})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(see
[
7
]
), where
n=\frac{m}{2}
and
D_{n}
is the dihedral group of order
2n
, it does not help in characterization as it involves the unknown factor
R_{u}\vee R_{v}
instead of
R
.

7.4.3
Connes-Størmer relative entropy

In this section, we investigate various relative entropies for the following quadruples

\begin{matrix}R_{u}&\subset&R\\
\cup&&\cup\\
R_{u}\cap R_{v}&\subset&R_{v}\end{matrix}\quad\mbox{ if }u\nsim v\,;\qquad\qquad\begin{matrix}&&R_{u}&\subset&R\\
&&\cup&&\cup\\
&&R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}&\subset&R_{v}\end{matrix}\quad\mbox{ if }u\sim v.

Most importantly, we obtain the exact value of
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
when
u\sim v
. Note that by
7.46
, it is clear that Theorem
6
in
[
43
]
is not applicable when
b\neq\pm ia
to find the value of
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
. However, it readily follows from
[
36
]
that
H(R|R_{u})=H(R|R_{v})=\log[R:R_{u}]=\log[R:R_{v}]=\log 4
, as spin model subfactors are irreducible. We give a pictorial roadmap in
Figure

16
to describe how we have reached at the stage of obtaining the exact value of relative entropy
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
when
u\sim v
.

Start with inequivalent
4\times 4

distinct complex Hadamard
matrices
u\mbox{ and }v
Obtain the spin model subfactors
R_{u}\mbox{ and }R_{v}\,
Identify the finite-dimensional grid for
\,R_{u}\cap R_{v}
via the spectral decomposition
Two cases appear,
either
u\sim v
or
u\nsim v\,
When
u\sim v,\,R_{u}\cap R_{v}\,
is a finite index and
irreducible subfactor of
R

When
u\nsim v,\,R_{u}\cap R_{v}\,
has infinite Pimsner-
Popa index in
R

The proper subfactor
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R\,
Legitimate lower
and upper bounds for
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
when
u\nsim v\,
Exact value of
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
when
u\sim v\,

Figure 16:
Roadmap for relative entropies

Proposition 7.49
.

For the pair of spin model subfactors
R_{u},R_{v}\subset R
, if
u\sim v
with
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1
, then we have the following,

(i)

H(R_{u}|R_{u}\cap R_{v})=H(R_{v}|R_{u}\cap R_{v})=\log m-\log 2\,,

(ii)

H(R|R_{u}\cap R_{v})=\log 2m
.

Proof
:
Let
u=u(a),\,v=u(b)
and
\theta=\overline{a}b
. Since
u\sim v
, there exists
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
such that
\,\theta^{\,m}=1
, and we have the subfactor
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
constructed in
7.32
such that
[R:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]=2m
. Recall from Theorem
4.4
and Corollary
4.5
in
[
36
]
that for a finite index subfactor
N\subset M
, we have
H(M|N)=\log[M:N]
if and only if the subfactor is extremal. Since
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
is constructed through the basic construction, it is extremal. Therefore,
H(R|R^{(m)}_{u,v})=\log 2m
and
H(R_{u}|R^{(m)}_{u,v})=\log[R_{u}:R^{(m)}_{u,v}]=\log\frac{2m}{4}
. Since
R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}
by
7.37
, we are done.

\Box

Now, recall the subfactor
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R
from
7.44
.

Lemma 7.50
.

We have
H(R|R_{u}\vee R_{v})=H(R_{u}\vee R_{v}|R_{u})=H(R_{u}\vee R_{v}|R_{v})=\log 2
. Moreover, when
u=u(a)
and
v=u(b)
are such that
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1
for some
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
(that is,
u\sim v
), we have
H\big(R_{u}\vee R_{v}|R_{u}\cap R_{v}\big)=\log m
.

Proof
:
Recall that for any irreducible subfactor
N\subset M
, we have
H(M|N)=\log[M:N]
by
[
36
]
. All the subfactors
R_{u}\vee R_{v}\subset R,\,R_{u}\subset R_{u}\vee R_{v},\,R_{v}\subset R_{u}\vee R_{v}
are of index
2
, and hence irreducible. This proves the first part. Now, given
u=u(a)\mbox{ and }v=u(b)
recall that if
(\overline{a}b)^{m}=1
for some
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
, then
R_{u}\cap R_{v}=R^{(m)}_{u,v}
is an irreducible subfactor of
R
with
[R:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=2m
(
7.32
,
7.36
and
7.37
). Hence,
[R_{u}\vee R_{v}:R_{u}\cap R_{v}]=m
. Since
R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u}\vee R_{v}
also becomes irreducible, we have
H\big(R_{u}\vee R_{v}|R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}\big)=\log m
.

\Box

Theorem 7.51
.

For the pair of spin model subfactors
R_{u},R_{v}\subset R
, we have the following.

(i)

If
\,u\sim v
, then
H(R_{u}|R_{v})=h(R_{u}|R_{v})=\log 2;

(ii)

If
\,u\nsim v
, then

0<\log 2+\frac{1}{8}\left(\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)\leq H(R_{u}|R_{v})\leq\log 2\,.

Proof
:
For given
u\mbox{ and }v
, existence of the intermediate subfactor
R_{u}\vee R_{v}
containing both
R_{u}\mbox{ and }R_{v}
immediately gives us the following,

\displaystyle H(R_{u}|R_{v})\leq H(R_{u}|R_{u}\vee R_{v})+H(R_{u}\vee R_{v}|R_{v})=H(R_{u}\vee R_{v}|R_{v})=\log 2

(7.32)

by
7.50
.

If
\,u\sim v
such that
\theta=\overline{a}b
satisfies
\theta^{\,m}=1
for some
m\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
, then consider the quadruple
\big(R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}\subset R_{u},R_{v}\subset R\big)
. By
7.36
, we have
R^{(m)}_{u,v}\subset R
irreducible. Hence, by
2.19
we get that
h(R_{u}|R_{v})=-\log\lambda(R_{u},R_{v})
and
H(R_{u}|R_{v})\geq-\log\lambda(R_{u},R_{v})
. By
7.8
, we have
\lambda(R_{u},R_{v})=1/2
. Part
(i)
now follows from
Equation

7.32
.

For part
(ii)
, we only need to provide the lower bound. Note that
H(R_{u}|R_{v})
is limit of an increasing sequence where the first term is
H\left(\mbox{Ad}_{u}\Delta_{4}|\mbox{Ad}_{v}\Delta_{4}\right)
. We claim the following,

\displaystyle 0<\log 2+\frac{1}{8}\left(\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)\leq H\left(u\Delta_{4}u^{*}|v\Delta_{4}v^{*}\right)\,.

(7.33)

Consider the partition
\gamma=\{uE_{11}u^{*},\ldots,uE_{44}u^{*}\}
of
I_{4}\in M_{4}
consisting of minimal projections. Then, by definition of relative entropy and using the fact that for
p\in(M_{4})_{+}
we have
\eta(p)=0
if and only if
p
is a projection (see
[
36
]
, for instance), we have the following,

\displaystyle H_{\gamma}\left(u\Delta_{4}u^{*}|v\Delta_{4}v^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{4}\tau\eta E_{v\Delta_{4}v^{*}}^{M_{4}}\left(uE_{jj}u^{*}\right)-\tau\eta E_{u\Delta_{4}u^{*}}^{M_{4}}\left(uE_{jj}u^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{4}\tau\eta\big(\mbox{Ad}_{v}E_{\Delta_{4}}^{M_{4}}\left(v^{*}uE_{jj}u^{*}v\right)\big)-\tau\eta\big(\mbox{Ad}_{u}E_{\Delta_{4}}^{M_{4}}\left(E_{jj}\right)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{4}\tau\eta\big(E_{\Delta_{4}}^{M_{4}}\left(v^{*}uE_{jj}u^{*}v\right)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{4}\frac{1}{4}\eta\left(|(v^{*}u)_{ij}|^{2}\right)\,,

where
(v^{*}u)_{ij}
denotes the
i,j
-entry of the matrix
v^{*}u
. Since
v^{*}u=p+q^{*}
, we get the following (recall few properties of
\eta
from the beginning of section
3
in
[
36
]
, for instance),

\displaystyle H_{\gamma}\left(u\Delta_{4}u^{*}|v\Delta_{4}v^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta\left(\frac{1}{4}|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)+\eta\left(\frac{1}{4}|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\eta\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{8}\left(\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log 2+\frac{1}{8}\left(\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)\,.

Finally, obeserve that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\left(\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\eta\left(|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{8}\eta(4)=-\log 2\,.

The inequality above is in fact strict. To see this, take
a\overline{b}=e^{i\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(-\pi,\pi)
, and observe that
\eta(1+\cos\gamma)+\eta(1-\cos\gamma)>\eta(2)
(since in our situation
b\neq\pm a
). Since
H=\sup_{\gamma}\,H_{\gamma}
, this gives us the required estimate in
Equation

7.33
.

\Box

Note that
\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}
is always negative. To see this, take
a\overline{b}=e^{i\gamma}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and observe that
\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}=-4\log 2+2\eta(1+\cos\gamma)+2\eta(1-\cos\gamma)
. On contrary, if we assume that there exists
\gamma
such that
2\eta(1+\cos\gamma)+2\eta(1-\cos\gamma)\geq 4\log 2
, then one arrives at the stage
\eta\big(\cos^{2}\frac{\gamma}{2}\big)+\eta\big(\sin^{2}\frac{\gamma}{2}\big)\geq\log 4
. However, the global maxima for
\eta
is
e^{-1}
, and therefore the left hand side can be at the best
2e^{-1}
, which is strictly smaller than
\log 4
. Hence, no such
\gamma
can exist and the quantity
\eta|1+a\overline{b}|^{2}+\eta|1-a\overline{b}|^{2}
is always negative.

Corollary 7.52
.

If
u\sim v
, then for the pair of spin model subfactors
R_{u},R_{v}\subset R
, we have
H(R_{u}|R_{v})=-\log\lambda(R_{u},R_{v})
.

Proof
:
Combine
7.8
and
7.51
.

\Box

We summarize all the relative entropies
H(\,.\,|\,.\,)
when
u\sim v
(i,e. when
\theta\in\Gamma
) in a
5\times 5
matrix form in
Table

2
.

\,H(\,.\,|\,.\,)\,\mbox{for }u\sim v\,

\,\,R\,\,

R_{u}

R_{v}

R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}

R_{u}\vee R_{v}

R

0

\log 4

\log 4

\log 2+\log m

\log 2

R_{u}

0

\log 2

\log m-\log 2

0

\log 2

0

\log m-\log 2

0

R^{(m)}_{u,v}=R_{u}\cap R_{v}

0

0

0

0

0
